Exercise 32 ★ Storage requirements

A grayscale image has $600\times800$ pixels.

  1. How many numbers does the approximation with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{k}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{40}$ building blocks need? What fraction of the original number is that?

  2. From which $k$ on does the approximation no longer save any storage?

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{40\cdot(600+800+1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{40\cdot1\,401}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{56\,040}$ numbers instead of $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{600\cdot800}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{480\,000}$, that is, about $11.7\,\%$.

(b) The approximation saves storage as long as $k\cdot1\,401<480\,000$, that is, for $k\le342$, because $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{342\cdot1\,401}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{479\,142}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{343\cdot1\,401}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{480\,543}$. From $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{k}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{343}$ on, the approximation needs more numbers than the image itself. The rank is at most $600$; there are no more than $600$ building blocks anyway.

The pseudoinverse

Exercise 33 ★ A left inverse

The least-squares line in Section 3.6 belongs to $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\Bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\1&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\Bigr)}$.

  1. Compute the left inverse $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}}$ and check $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf F\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{I}}$.

  2. Apply $\mathbf F$ to $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{y}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2,\,4)^{\top}}$ and compare with Section 3.6.

  3. Read off from the rows of $\mathbf F$ how the intercept and the slope of the least-squares line arise for arbitrary measured values $y_1,y_2,y_3$.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}3&3\\3&5\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$ with the inverse $\tfrac16\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}5&-3\\-3&3\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, so

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac16\begin{pmatrix}5&-3\\-3&3\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\0&1&2\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac16\begin{pmatrix}5&2&-1\\-3&0&3\end{pmatrix},}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf F\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac16\begin{pmatrix}5+2-1&2-2\\-3+3&6\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{I} .}$$

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf F(1,\,2,\,4)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac16(5+4-4,\ -3+12)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\tfrac56,\,\tfrac32)^{\top}}$, the least-squares line from Section 3.6.

(c) For arbitrary measured values, the intercept is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\hat x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac16(5y_1+2y_2-y_3)}$ and the slope is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\hat x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac12(y_3-y_1)}$. The weights of the intercept add up to $1$: if all measured values are equal, the intercept is this value. For three equally spaced data points, the slope depends only on the two outer points; the middle point affects only the height of the line.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 34 ★★ Shortest solutions

  1. Determine the pseudoinverse of $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$ and $\mathbf{x}^+$ for $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{y}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,3)^{\top}}$. Check that $\mathbf{x}^+$ is a best approximate solution and that no shorter one exists.

  2. Determine the shortest solution of $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1+2x_2+2x_3}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9}$. Compare its length with that of the solution $(9,\,0,\,0)^{\top}$.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\,\mathbf{q}\mathbf{q}^{\top}}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{q}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,1)^{\top}/\sqrt2}$, symmetric and positive semidefinite, so this is the compact decomposition with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{d_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{u}_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{v}_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{q}}$. Hence $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac12\mathbf{q}\mathbf{q}^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac14\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{x}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac14(4,\,4)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,1)^{\top}}$. We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,2)^{\top}}$, the projection of $(1,\,3)^{\top}$ onto the line in the direction of $(1,\,1)^{\top}$, because $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\tfrac{1+3}2(1,\,1)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,2)^{\top}}$. The best approximate solutions are all $\mathbf{x}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1+x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$, that is, $(1+s,\ 1-s)^{\top}$ with the squared length $2+2s^2$. The shortest is the one at $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{s}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$, and that is $\mathbf{x}^+$.

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1\ \ 2\ \ 2)}$ has $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf G}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac19(1,\,2,\,2)^{\top}}$, and the shortest solution is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf G\cdot9}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2,\,2)^{\top}}$ with length $3$. The solution $(9,\,0,\,0)^{\top}$ has length $9$. The shortest solution points in the direction of the normal vector $(1,\,2,\,2)^{\top}$ of the solution plane.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 35 ★★★ What the pseudoinverse projects

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}}$ with rank $r$, and let $\mathbf{U}_r$, $\mathbf{V}_r$ contain the first $r$ columns of $\mathbf{U}$ and $\mathbf{V}$.

  1. Show $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{U}_r\mathbf{U}_r^{\top}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^+\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{V}_r\mathbf{V}_r^{\top}}$. Onto which spaces do these matrices project?

  2. Conclude $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^+\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^+\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}^+}$.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{D}^+\mathbf{U}^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^+\mathbf{U}^{\top}}$. The $m\times m$ matrix $\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^+$ has $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{d_j\cdot\tfrac1{d_j}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ in the first $r$ diagonal positions and zeros elsewhere. Hence $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sum_{j\le r}\mathbf{u}_j\mathbf{u}_j^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{U}_r\mathbf{U}_r^{\top}}$, by (4.2) the orthogonal projection onto the span of $\mathbf{u}_1,\dots,\mathbf{u}_r$, the column space of $\mathbf{A}$. Likewise, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^+\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{V}\mathbf{D}^+\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{V}_r\mathbf{V}_r^{\top}}$, the projection onto the span of $\mathbf{v}_1,\dots,\mathbf{v}_r$, the column space of $\mathbf{A}^{\top}$; this is the orthogonal complement of the null space.

(b) Every column of $\mathbf{A}$ lies in the column space, and the projection onto it leaves it fixed: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^+\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\mathbf{U}_r\mathbf{U}_r^{\top})\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}}$. Likewise, every column of $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sum_{j\le r}\tfrac1{d_j}\mathbf{v}_j\mathbf{u}_j^{\top}}$ lies in the span of $\mathbf{v}_1,\dots,\mathbf{v}_r$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^+\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\mathbf{V}_r\mathbf{V}_r^{\top})\mathbf{A}^+}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}^+}$.

Go to the place in the article

The condition number

Exercise 36 ★ Reading off condition numbers

Determine the condition number of

  1. $\operatorname{diag}(2,\ 0.5)$ and $\operatorname{diag}(-3,\ 3)$;

  2. the rotation matrix for the angle $30^\circ$;

  3. $5\mathbf{A}$ for $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}2&3\\0&2\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$. Compare the determinants of $\mathbf{A}$ and $5\mathbf{A}$.

Solution

(a) $\operatorname{diag}(2,\,0.5)$ has the singular values $2$ and $0.5$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4}$. By Section 9.7, $\operatorname{diag}(-3,\,3)$ has the singular values $3$ and $3$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, even though the eigenvalues have different signs.

(b) A rotation matrix is orthogonal, and all its singular values are $1$: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$.

(c) $5\mathbf{A}$ has the singular values $20$ and $5$, so again $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4}$. The determinant, by contrast, grows from $4$ to $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\det(5\mathbf{A})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{25\cdot4}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{100}$.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 37 ★★ Determinant 1, yet ill-conditioned

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}10&7\\7&5\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$.

  1. Compute $\det\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{A}^{-1}$. Solve $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(17,\,12)^{\top}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(17,\ 12.1)^{\top}}$.

  2. Compute the relative changes of $\mathbf{y}$ and $\mathbf{x}$ and their ratio.

  3. Compute $\kappa(\mathbf{A})$ and check (12.2). How far is $\mathbf{A}$ from the nearest singular matrix?

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\det\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{50-49}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^{-1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}5&-7\\-7&10\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$. Hence $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^{-1}(17,\,12)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(85-84,\ -119+120)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,1)^{\top}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^{-1}(17,\ 12.1)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(85-84.7,\ -119+121)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.3,\ 2)^{\top}}$.

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\Delta\mathbf{y}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.1/\sqrt{433}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.1/20.809}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.48\,\%}$. Further, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\Delta\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(-0.7,\ 1)^{\top}}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\Delta\mathbf{x}\rVert/\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{1.49}/\sqrt2}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1.2207/1.4142}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{86.3\,\%}$. The ratio is about $180$.

(c) $\mathbf{A}$ is symmetric with trace $15$ and determinant $1$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lambda}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac{15\pm\sqrt{221}}2}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{14.933}$ and $0.0670$, both positive. By Section 9.7, these are the singular values, and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa(\mathbf{A})}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{14.933/0.0670}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{223}$. The bound (12.2) is satisfied: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{86.3\,\%\le223\cdot0.48\,\%}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{107\,\%}$. The distance to the nearest singular matrix is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{d_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.067}$, or $1/223$ in relative terms. The determinant $1$ reveals nothing of this.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 38 ★★★ Rules for the condition number

Let $\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ be invertible and $\mathbf{Q}$ orthogonal. Show:

  1. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa(\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{A})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\kappa(\mathbf{A})}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa(\mathbf{A}^{-1})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\kappa(\mathbf{A})}$;

  2. $|\lambda|/|\mu|\le\kappa(\mathbf{A})$ for all eigenvalues $\lambda$ and $\mu$ of $\mathbf{A}$;

  3. if $\mathbf{A}$ is symmetric, then $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa(\mathbf{A})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{|\lambda|_{\max}/|\lambda|_{\min}}$, the ratio of the eigenvalue of largest magnitude to the eigenvalue of smallest magnitude.

Solution

(a) By Exercise 29, $\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{A}$ has the same singular values as $\mathbf{A}$, and hence the same condition number. From $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}}$ it follows that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^{-1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{V}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{U}^{\top}}$, a singular value decomposition with the singular values $1/d_j$. The largest is $1/d_n$, the smallest $1/d_1$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\kappa(\mathbf{A}^{-1})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac{1/d_n}{1/d_1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac{d_1}{d_n}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\kappa(\mathbf{A})}$.

(b) For an eigenvector $\mathbf{v}$ belonging to $\lambda$, (9.5) gives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{d_n\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert\le\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{|\lambda|\,\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert\le d_1\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert}$, so $d_n\le|\lambda|\le d_1$; this holds for complex eigenvectors as well, as shown in Section 9.8. The same holds for $\mu$. Hence $|\lambda|/|\mu|\le d_1/d_n$.

(c) For symmetric $\mathbf{A}$, by Section 9.7, the singular values are the magnitudes $|\lambda_i|$. The largest singular value is therefore $|\lambda|_{\max}$, the smallest $|\lambda|_{\min}$.

Chapter 3: Derivative, Gradient and Chain Rule

The derivative

Exercise 1 ★ Secant lines and the tangent line

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^2}$.

  1. Compute the difference quotient at the point $3$ for $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{h}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{h}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.1}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{h}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-0.1}$.

  2. Show that the difference quotient at the point $3$ equals $6+h$, and determine $f'(3)$.

  3. State the tangent line at the point $3$ and use it to compute an approximation for $3.02^2$. How large is the error?

Solution

(a) With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(3)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9}$,

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{4^2-9}{1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{7,}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{3.1^2-9}{0.1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{9.61-9}{0.1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{6.1,}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{2.9^2-9}{-0.1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{8.41-9}{-0.1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5.9 .}$$

The secant line to the right is steeper and the one to the left is flatter than the tangent line, because the graph of the parabola curves upward.

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(3+h)^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9+6h+h^2}$, so the difference quotient is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{6h+h^2}{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{6+h}$. As $h\to0$, it tends to $6$ from both sides; thus $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(3)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{6}$, in agreement with the rule $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(x^2)'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x}$.

(c) The tangent line is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{t(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9+6\,(x-3)}$. It gives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{3.02^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9+6\cdot0.02}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9.12}$. The exact value is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{3.02^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9.1204}$. The error $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{0.0004}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.02^2}$ is exactly $h^2$, because $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(3+h)-9-6h}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{h^2}$. It vanishes faster than $h$, as the linear approximation promises.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 2 ★ Differentiating with the rules

Differentiate and evaluate at the given point.

  1. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4x^3-2x+7}$ at the point $1$;

  2. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^2\,\mathrm{e}^x}$ at the points $0$ and $1$;

  3. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\dfrac{x}{1+x^2}}$ at the points $0$ and $1$. What does the result at the point $1$ mean for the graph?

Solution

(a) Sum rule and constant multiple rule together with the power rule: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{12x^2-2}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{10}$.

(b) Product rule with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^2}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{v}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathrm{e}^x}$: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x\,\mathrm{e}^x+x^2\,\mathrm{e}^x}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2x+x^2)\,\mathrm{e}^x}$. So $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(0)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{3\mathrm{e}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{8.155}$.

(c) Quotient rule with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{v}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1+x^2}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{v'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x}$:

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{1\cdot(1+x^2)-x\cdot2x}{(1+x^2)^2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{1-x^2}{(1+x^2)^2},}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(0)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1,}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0 .}$$

At the point $1$, the graph has a horizontal tangent line. Its highest point lies there: for all $x$, $\frac{x}{1+x^2}\le\frac12$, because $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{1+x^2-2x}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(x-1)^2\ge0}$, and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac12}$.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 3 ★★ Square roots under the magnifying glass

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt x}$.

  1. Compute $f'(x)$ and $f''(x)$ with the power rule.

  2. Use the linear and the quadratic approximation at the point $4$ to compute an approximate value for $\sqrt{4.1}$. Compare both with a calculator.

  3. Does the tangent line lie above or below the graph? Justify your answer with the sign of $f''(4)$.

Solution

(a) With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sqrt x}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^{1/2}}$, we get $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac12x^{-1/2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{2\sqrt x}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f''(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-\tfrac14x^{-3/2}}$.

(b) At the point $4$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(4)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(4)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac14}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f''(4)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac18}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-\tfrac1{32}}$. With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{h}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.1}$:

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\text{linear: }2+\tfrac14\cdot0.1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2.025,}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\text{quadratic: }2.025-\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac1{32}\cdot0.01}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2.025-0.00015625}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2.02484375 .}$$

The calculator gives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sqrt{4.1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2.0248457}$. The error of the linear approximation is about $1.5\cdot10^{-4}$, that of the quadratic one only about $1.9\cdot10^{-6}$.

(c) Since $f''(4)<0$, the graph bends downward away from the tangent line; the tangent line lies above the graph. That is why the linear approximation $2.025$ is too large.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 4 ★★ Kink or no kink

  1. Use the difference quotient to show that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x\,|x|}$ is differentiable at the point $0$, and determine $g'(0)$.

  2. Determine $g'(x)$ for $x>0$ and for $x<0$. Does $g'$ itself have a derivative at the point $0$?

  3. The function $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{m(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\max(x,\,0)}$ returns $x$ if $x$ is positive and $0$ otherwise. Where is it differentiable, and what values does its derivative take there?

Solution

(a) We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g(0)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{g(h)-g(0)}{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{h\,|h|}{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{|h|}$. This tends to $0$ from both sides, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g'(0)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$. The absolute value alone has a kink, but the factor $x$ flattens the two branches at the origin.

(b) For $x>0$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x}$; for $x<0$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-x^2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-2x}$. Together with (a), $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2|x|}$ for all $x$. This function has a kink at $0$; its difference quotient $2|h|/h$ is $\pm2$. So $g$ is differentiable once, but not twice.

(c) For $x>0$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{m(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{m'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$; for $x<0$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{m(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{m'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$. At the point $0$, the difference quotient is $1$ for $h>0$ and $0$ for $h<0$; there is no derivative there. The graph has a kink at $0$.

Go to the place in the article

The chain rule

Exercise 5 ★ Bell curve

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathrm{e}^{-x^2}}$.

  1. Determine the inner and the outer function and compute $f'(x)$.

  2. Compute $f'(0)$, $f'(1)$ and $f'(-1)$ and interpret the signs on the graph.

Solution

(a) The inner function is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-x^2}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-2x}$, the outer one is $\mathrm{e}^u$ with the derivative $\mathrm{e}^u$. By the chain rule, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathrm{e}^{-x^2}\cdot(-2x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-2x\,\mathrm{e}^{-x^2}}$.

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(0)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$: the graph has a horizontal tangent line at the point $0$; its peak is there. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-2/\mathrm{e}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-0.7358}$: to the right of the peak, it falls. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f'(-1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2/\mathrm{e}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.7358}$: to the left of the peak, it rises. Because $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(-x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{f(x)}$, the slopes at the points $\pm1$ are equal in magnitude and opposite in sign.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 6 ★★ A chain with three links

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{y}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{1+x^4}}$.

  1. Decompose the function into three links $u_1$, $u_2$, $y$, and at the point $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ compute first all intermediate values, then all factors.

  2. Compute $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{d} x}$ at the point $1$ as the product of the factors, and check it with the chain rule in a single step.

Solution

(a) The links are $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^4}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1+u_1}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{y}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{u_2}}$. At the point $1$, the intermediate values are $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{y}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt2}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1.4142}$. The factors are $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{\mathrm{d} u_1}{\mathrm{d} x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4x^3}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{\mathrm{d} u_2}{\mathrm{d} u_1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{d} u_2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{2\sqrt{u_2}}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{2\sqrt2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.3536}$.

(b) The product is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{4\cdot1\cdot\frac1{2\sqrt2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac2{\sqrt2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt2}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1.4142}$. In a single step: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{y'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{2\sqrt{1+x^4}}\cdot4x^3}$, so at the point $1$ it is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac4{2\sqrt2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt2}$.

Exercise 7 ★★ Properties of the logistic function

  1. Use (2.4) and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sigma(-x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1-\sigma(x)}$ to show that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sigma'(-x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sigma'(x)}$. What does this mean for the graph of $\sigma'$?

  2. Compute $\sigma(1)$ and $\sigma'(1)$ to four decimal places.

  3. Compute the second derivative $\sigma''$, expressed in terms of $\sigma$, and determine its sign for $x>0$ and for $x<0$. What does this say about the curvature of the graph of $\sigma$?

Solution

(a) With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sigma(-x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1-\sigma(x)}$, we get $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sigma'(-x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sigma(-x)\,\bigl(1-\sigma(-x)\bigr)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(1-\sigma(x)\bigr)\,\sigma(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sigma'(x)}$. The graph of $\sigma'$ is symmetric about the vertical axis, as Fig. 5 shows.

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathrm{e}^{-1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.3679}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sigma(1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1/1.3679}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.7311}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sigma'(1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.7311\cdot0.2689}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.1966}$.

(c) With the product rule and the chain rule: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sigma''}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\sigma-\sigma^2)'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sigma'-2\sigma\sigma'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sigma'\,(1-2\sigma)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sigma(1-\sigma)(1-2\sigma)}$. For $x>0$, $\sigma>\tfrac12$, so $1-2\sigma<0$ and $\sigma''<0$: the graph bends downward there. For $x<0$, $\sigma''>0$, and it bends upward. At the point $0$, the curvature changes its sign; the slope is largest there.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 8 ★★★ The derivative of an inverse function

For $x>0$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathrm{e}^{\ln x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$.

  1. Differentiate both sides with respect to $x$ using the chain rule and conclude that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(\ln x)'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1/x}$, assuming that $\ln$ is differentiable.

  2. Proceed in the same way with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(\sqrt x)^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$ and compare with the power rule.

  3. The inverse function of the logistic function is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\ell(p)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\ln\frac{p}{1-p}}$ for $0<p<1$. Check that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\ell(\sigma(x))}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$, and compute $\ell'(p)$ once directly and once via $\sigma'$.

Solution

(a) The left-hand side is a composition with the inner function $\ln x$ and the outer function $\mathrm{e}^u$. By the chain rule, its derivative is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathrm{e}^{\ln x}\cdot(\ln x)'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x\cdot(\ln x)'}$. The right-hand side has the derivative $1$. So $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x\,(\ln x)'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(\ln x)'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1/x}$.

(b) From $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(\sqrt x)^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$ it follows that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2\sqrt x\cdot(\sqrt x)'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(\sqrt x)'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{2\sqrt x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac12x^{-1/2}}$, exactly the power rule with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{n}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac12}$.

(c) We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{1-\sigma(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-x}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{-x}}}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{\sigma(x)}{1-\sigma(x)}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{\mathrm{e}^{-x}}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathrm{e}^x}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\ell(\sigma(x))}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\ln\mathrm{e}^x}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$. Directly, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\ell(p)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\ln p-\ln(1-p)}$ and

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\ell'(p)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1p+\frac1{1-p}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{1}{p\,(1-p)} .}$$

Via $\sigma'$: differentiating $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\ell(\sigma(x))}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$ gives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\ell'(\sigma(x))\cdot\sigma'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\ell'(p)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{\sigma'(x)}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{p(1-p)}}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{p}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sigma(x)}$. Where $\sigma$ is flat, its inverse function is steep.

Partial derivatives and the gradient

Exercise 9 ★ Partial derivatives and gradients

Compute the partial derivatives and the gradient at the given point.

  1. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{3x_1-2x_2+5}$ at the point $(4,\,-1)$;

  2. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1\,\mathrm{e}^{x_2}}$ at the points $(2,\,0)$ and $(1,\,1)$;

  3. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1x_2+x_2x_3+x_3x_1}$ at the point $(1,\,2,\,3)$.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{3}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-2}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(3,\,-2)^{\top}}$ at every point.

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathrm{e}^{x_2}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1\mathrm{e}^{x_2}}$. At the point $(2,\,0)$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2)^{\top}}$; at the point $(1,\,1)$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\mathrm{e},\,\mathrm{e})^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2.718,\ 2.718)^{\top}}$.

(c) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_2+x_3}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1+x_3}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_3f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1+x_2}$; so at the point $(1,\,2,\,3)$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(5,\,4,\,3)^{\top}}$.

Exercise 10 ★★ Linear approximation with two variables

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1\,\mathrm{e}^{x_2}}$.

  1. Use the linear approximation at the point $(2,\,0)$ to compute an approximate value for $f(2.1,\ -0.05)$, and compare it with the exact value.

  2. Which of the two changes contributes more to the change in $f$? Why do they cancel exactly in the linear approximation but not in the exact value?

Solution

(a) We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(2,0)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(2,0)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2)^{\top}}$ by Exercise 9. With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.1,\ -0.05)^{\top}}$:

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(2.1,\ -0.05)}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2+1\cdot0.1+2\cdot(-0.05)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2+0.1-0.1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2.0 .}$$

The exact value is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2.1\,\mathrm{e}^{-0.05}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2.1\cdot0.951229}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1.99758}$. The error is about $0.0024$.

(b) Both changes contribute equally, $+0.1$ and $-0.1$, and cancel in the linear approximation. In the exact value, however, the variables influence each other: if $x_1$ grows, $f$ responds more strongly to $x_2$, because $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1\mathrm{e}^{x_2}}$ grows with $x_1$. The decrease in $x_2$ therefore has a somewhat stronger effect than the linear approximation assumes. Such interactions are products of two changes, here about $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{0.1\cdot(-0.05)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-0.005}$, and belong to the second-order terms that the linear approximation leaves out. Section 8 computes them (Exercise 29).

Exercise 11 ★★ Contour lines and cross-section curves

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1^2x_2+x_2^2}$ as in (3.1).

  1. What is the value of $f$ at the point $(2,\,1)$? Give another point on the same contour line with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$.

  2. Compute $\nabla f(2,1)$ and the equations of the two cross-section curves through $(2,\,1)$. Check their slopes.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(2,1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4\cdot1+1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5}$. With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$, the contour line requires $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_2^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5}$, so, for example, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(0,\ \sqrt5)}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0,\ 2.236)}$ lies on it.

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(2,1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2\cdot2\cdot1,\ 4+2\cdot1)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(4,\,6)^{\top}}$. The cross-section curve along $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(x_1,1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1^2+1}$, with slope $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4}$ at $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$. The cross-section curve along $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$ is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(2,x_2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4x_2+x_2^2}$, with slope $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{4+2x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{6}$ at $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$. Both slopes agree with the components of the gradient.

Exercise 12 ★★★ Continuous but not differentiable

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(x_1,x_2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{|x_1x_2|}}$.

  1. Show that both partial derivatives exist at the origin and are $0$.

  2. Using $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(t,\,t)^{\top}}$, show that the quotient in (3.5) with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{0}}$ does not tend to $0$. Why does it follow that $f$ is not differentiable at the origin, even though $f$ is continuous there?

Solution

(a) On both axes, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$. Hence $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{f(h,0)-f(0,0)}{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$ for every $h\ne0$, and likewise for the second variable. Both partial derivatives at the origin are $0$.

(b) If $f$ were differentiable at the origin, then by the theorem in Section 3.5 the vector in (3.5) would have to be the gradient $\mathbf{0}$. With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(t,\,t)^{\top}}$, however, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{h})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{t^2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{|t|}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\mathbf{h}\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt2\,|t|}$, so

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\frac{f(\mathbf{h})-f(\mathbf{0})-\mathbf{0}^{\top}\mathbf{h}}{\lVert\mathbf{h}\rVert}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{|t|}{\sqrt2\,|t|}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac1{\sqrt2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.707 ,}$$

for every $t\ne0$, however small. The quotient does not tend to $0$, so $f$ is not differentiable. Nevertheless, $f$ is continuous, because $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{|f(\mathbf{x})|}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{|x_1|\,|x_2|}\le\max(|x_1|,|x_2|)\le\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert}$ tends to $0$ as $\mathbf{x}$ tends to $\mathbf{0}$. The terrain has no gap at the origin, but along the diagonal it has a crease that no plane can hug.

Go to the place in the article

Directional derivative and steepest ascent

Exercise 13 ★ Slopes in many directions

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1^2+4x_2^2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,1)^{\top}}$.

  1. Compute the directional derivatives in the directions $\mathbf{e}_1$, $\mathbf{e}_2$, $(1,\,1)^{\top}/\sqrt2$ and $(-1,\,1)^{\top}/\sqrt2$.

  2. Compare with $\lVert\nabla f(\mathbf{x})\rVert$. Which of the four directions is closest to the steepest one?

Solution

(a) We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(1,1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,8)^{\top}}$. By (4.2):

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{D_{\mathbf{e}_1}f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2,}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{D_{\mathbf{e}_2}f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{8,}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{D_{(1,1)/\sqrt2}f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{2+8}{\sqrt2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{7.071,}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{D_{(-1,1)/\sqrt2}f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\frac{-2+8}{\sqrt2}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4.243 .}$$

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\nabla f\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{68}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{8.246}$; none of the slopes is larger, as (4.3) requires. Closest to the steepest direction is $\mathbf{e}_2$, with $8$. The angle between $\mathbf{e}_2$ and $\nabla f$ has the cosine $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{8/\sqrt{68}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.970}$, so it is about $14^\circ$; for $(1,\,1)^{\top}/\sqrt2$ it is about $31^\circ$.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 14 ★★ A hyperbola as a contour line

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1x_2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,1)^{\top}}$.

  1. Compute $\nabla f(\mathbf{x})$, the steepest direction and the slope in it.

  2. The contour line through $\mathbf{x}$ is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2/x_1}$. Compute the slope of this curve at $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$, write its tangent direction as a vector and check that it is perpendicular to $\nabla f(\mathbf{x})$.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(x_2,\,x_1)^{\top}}$, so at the point $(2,\,1)$ it is $(1,\,2)^{\top}$. The steepest direction is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(1,\,2)^{\top}/\sqrt5}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.447,\ 0.894)^{\top}}$, and the slope in it is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sqrt5}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2.236}$.

(b) The contour line $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2/x_1}$ has the slope $-2/x_1^2$, so $-\tfrac12$ at $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$. A tangent direction is $(1,\,-\tfrac12)^{\top}$ or, multiplied by $2$, $(2,\,-1)^{\top}$. We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{(1,\,2)(2,\,-1)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2-2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$: the gradient is perpendicular to the contour line.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 15 ★★ Linear functions

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+b}$ with a fixed $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^d$, $\mathbf{a}\ne\mathbf{0}$, and a number $b$.

  1. Show that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{a}}$ at every point. What are the contour lines for $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{d}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$?

  2. For the apartments from Chapter 1, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\hat y}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+b}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{w}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(3,\,10,\,-4)^{\top}}$, where $x_1$ is the floor area in $\mathrm m^2$, $x_2$ the number of rooms and $x_3$ the distance to the center in km. Which change $\mathbf{u}$ of the features with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ increases the prediction most, and by how much?

  3. If you give the floor area in $10\,\mathrm m^2$, the weight $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{w_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{3}$ becomes $30$. What is the steepest direction now? What does this teach you about the theorem on steepest ascent?

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_k(\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+b)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{a_k}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{a}}$ everywhere. For $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{d}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2}$, the contour lines are the lines $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{a_1x_1+a_2x_2+b}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{c}$; they are parallel to one another and perpendicular to $\mathbf{a}$.

(b) The gradient with respect to $\mathbf{x}$ is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{w}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(3,\,10,\,-4)^{\top}}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\mathbf{w}\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{9+100+16}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{125}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{11.18}$. The steepest change of length $1$ is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{u}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{w}/\sqrt{125}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.268,\ 0.894,\ -0.358)^{\top}}$, and it increases the prediction by about $11.18$ thousand euros: a little more floor area, considerably more rooms and a little closer to the center.

(c) In the new units, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{w}'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(30,\,10,\,-4)^{\top}}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\mathbf{w}'\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sqrt{1016}}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{31.87}$, and the steepest direction is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{w}'/\lVert\mathbf{w}'\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.941,\ 0.314,\ -0.125)^{\top}}$: now it lies almost entirely in the floor area. The same apartment, the same prices, but a different steepest direction. The theorem on steepest ascent compares steps of equal Euclidean length in the chosen coordinates, and what “length $1$” means depends on the units, just like the distances in Chapter 1, Section 4.2. For features in different units, the steepest direction becomes meaningful only after the standardization from Chapter 1, Section 4.5.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 16 ★★★ When does the steepest descent point to the center?

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{a\,x_1^2+b\,x_2^2}$ with numbers $a,b>0$.

  1. Show: $-\nabla f(\mathbf{x})$ is a positive multiple of $-\mathbf{x}$ if and only if $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{a}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{b}$ or $\mathbf{x}$ lies on one of the axes ($\mathbf{x}\ne\mathbf{0}$).

  2. For $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{a}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{b}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4}$, compute the angle between $-\nabla f$ and $-\mathbf{x}$ at the points $(1,\,1)$, $(2,\,1)$ and $(4,\,1)$. How does it change as you move outward along the line $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$?

Solution

(a) We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{-\nabla f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(-2ax_1,\,-2bx_2)^{\top}}$. This vector equals $c\,(-x_1,\,-x_2)^{\top}$ with some $c>0$ if and only if $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2ax_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{c\,x_1}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2bx_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{c\,x_2}$. If both coordinates are nonzero, it follows that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2a}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{c}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2b}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{a}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{b}$. If $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{c}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2b}$ suffices; if $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{c}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2a}$ suffices. So for a round bowl the steepest descent points to the origin everywhere, for an elongated one only on the axes.

(b) For $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{a}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{b}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{4}$, we have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{-\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(-2x_1,\,-8x_2)^{\top}}$:

$$\begin{aligned} (1,\,1)&:\ \cos\varphi=\frac{2+8}{\sqrt{68}\,\sqrt2}\approx0.857,\ \varphi\approx31.0^\circ;\\ (2,\,1)&:\ \cos\varphi=\frac{8+8}{\sqrt{80}\,\sqrt5}=0.8,\ \varphi\approx36.9^\circ;\\ (4,\,1)&:\ \cos\varphi=\frac{32+8}{\sqrt{128}\,\sqrt{17}}\approx0.857,\ \varphi\approx31.0^\circ . \end{aligned}$$

Along the line $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$, the angle first increases from $0^\circ$ at $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$ and then decreases again; far out, the point lies almost in the direction of the $x_1$-axis, and there the steepest descent again points almost to the center.

The Jacobian matrix

Exercise 17 ★ Computing Jacobian matrices

Compute the Jacobian matrix at the given point.

  1. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(x_1+x_2,\ x_1-x_2,\ x_1x_2)^{\top}}$ at the point $(3,\,1)$;

  2. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\mathrm{e}^{x_1},\,\mathrm{e}^{x_2},\,\mathrm{e}^{x_3})^{\top}}$ at the point $(0,\,1,\,-1)$;

  3. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1^2x_2+x_2^2}$ from (3.1) at the point $(1,\,2)$. What shape does the matrix have, and what is it?

Solution

(a) The Jacobian matrix has three rows and two columns:

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\\x_2&x_1\end{pmatrix},}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(3,1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}.}$$

(b) The function is elementwise; by (5.4), $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\operatorname{diag}(\mathrm{e}^0,\,\mathrm{e}^1,\,\mathrm{e}^{-1})}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\operatorname{diag}(1,\ 2.718,\ 0.368)}$.

(c) A function with one output has a $1\times2$ Jacobian matrix, the gradient as a row: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_f(1,2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(4\ \ 5)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\nabla f(1,2)^{\top}}$.

Exercise 18 ★★ Linear approximation of a map of the plane

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(x_1^2-x_2,\ x_1x_2)^{\top}}$.

  1. Compute $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(2,1)$ and use it to find an approximate value for $\mathbf{g}(2.05,\ 0.9)$. Compare with the exact value.

  2. How must you shift the inputs so that the first output changes but the second, in linear approximation, does not change at all? Give such a displacement $\mathbf{h}$.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}2x_1&-1\\x_2&x_1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(2,1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}4&-1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$, and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(2,1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(3,\,2)^{\top}}$. With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.05,\ -0.1)^{\top}}$,

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}0.2+0.1\\0.05-0.2\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}0.3\\-0.15\end{pmatrix},}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(2.05,\ 0.9)}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}3.3\\1.85\end{pmatrix}.}$$

The exact value is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(2.05,\ 0.9)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(4.2025-0.9,\ 1.845)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(3.3025,\ 1.845)^{\top}}$.

(b) In linear approximation, the second output changes by $h_1+2h_2$; this is zero for $\mathbf{h}$ in the direction $(2,\,-1)^{\top}$. For a step by exactly this vector, the first output then changes by $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{4\cdot2-1\cdot(-1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{9}$. So both inputs must change: $x_1$ grows and $x_2$ falls half as much, for example $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.02,\ -0.01)^{\top}}$ with the effect $(0.09,\ 0)^{\top}$.

Exercise 19 ★★★ How much $\mathbf{g}$ enlarges areas

Let $\mathbf{g}$ be as in (5.1).

  1. Compute the images of the four corners of the square with the corners $(1,\,2)$, $(1.1,\ 2)$, $(1.1,\ 2.1)$ and $(1,\ 2.1)$.

  2. The images almost form a parallelogram. Estimate its area using the images of the two edges that start at $\mathbf{g}(1,2)$, and compare with $|\det\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(1,2)|\cdot0.01$.

Solution

(a) With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(x_1x_2,\ 2x_1+x_2^2)^{\top}}$:

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(1,2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}2\\6\end{pmatrix},\quad \mathbf{g}(1.1,\,2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}2.2\\6.2\end{pmatrix},\quad \mathbf{g}(1.1,\,2.1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}2.31\\6.61\end{pmatrix},\quad \mathbf{g}(1,\,2.1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}2.1\\6.41\end{pmatrix}.}$$

(b) The images of the two edges that start at $\mathbf{g}(1,2)$ are $(0.2,\ 0.2)^{\top}$ and $(0.1,\ 0.41)^{\top}$. By Chapter 1, Section 14.1, the parallelogram they span has the area $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{|0.2\cdot0.41-0.1\cdot0.2|}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.062}$. The Jacobian matrix predicts $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{|\det\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(1,2)|\cdot0.01}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{6\cdot0.01}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.06}$. The discrepancy arises because the square is not arbitrarily small; the fourth corner, at $(2.31,\ 6.61)$, also lies only almost where a true parallelogram would have it, at $(2.3,\ 6.61)$.

The chain rule for several variables

Exercise 20 ★ Chain rule with Jacobian matrices

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(x_1+x_2,\ x_1x_2)^{\top}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{u})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{u_1^2-u_2}$.

  1. At the point $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,3)^{\top}}$, compute the intermediate value $\mathbf{u}$, the Jacobian matrix $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(\mathbf{x})$ and $\nabla f(\mathbf{u})$.

  2. Compute $\nabla(f\circ\mathbf{g})(\mathbf{x})$ with (6.3) and check it with the written-out function. What would you get without transposing?

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{u}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{g}(1,3)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(4,\,3)^{\top}}$. The Jacobian matrix is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\x_2&x_1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$, so at the point $(1,\,3)$ it is $\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\3&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$. The gradient of the outer function is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(\mathbf{u})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2u_1,\,-1)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(8,\,-1)^{\top}}$.

(b) By (6.3):

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla(f\circ\mathbf{g})(1,3)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}1&3\\1&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}8\\-1\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}8-3\\8-1\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}5\\7\end{pmatrix}.}$$

Check: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(x_1+x_2)^2-x_1x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1^2+x_1x_2+x_2^2}$ has the partial derivatives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2x_1+x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{x_1+2x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{7}$. Without transposing, you would get $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{g}}\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(8-1,\ 24-1)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(7,\,23)^{\top}}$, which is a wrong result.

Exercise 21 ★★ Three paths

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{g_1(x)\,g_2(x)\,g_3(x)}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g_1(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g_2(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g_3(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^3}$.

  1. Draw the network of paths from $x$ via $u_1,u_2,u_3$ to $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{u_1u_2u_3}$, and write its factor on each edge.

  2. Compute $F'(x)$ as a sum over the three paths and compare with the derivative of $x^6$.

Solution

(a) From $x$, three edges lead to $u_1$, $u_2$, $u_3$ with the factors $1$, $2x$ and $3x^2$. From each $u_i$, one edge leads to $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{u_1u_2u_3}$; its factor is the product of the other two: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u_2u_3}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^5}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u_1u_3}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^4}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{u_1u_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^3}$.

(b) Summed over the three paths:

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F'(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^5\cdot1+x^4\cdot2x+x^3\cdot3x^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^5+2x^5+3x^5}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{6x^5 .}$$

This is the derivative of $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F(x)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^6}$. The path via $u_k$ contributes $k\,x^5$, because $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{g_k}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x^k}$ has the derivative $k\,x^{k-1}$ and the other two factors together give $x^{6-k}$. So the product rule for three factors is the sum over three paths.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 22 ★★ A curve within a contour line

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1^2+4x_2^2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\sqrt5\cos t,\ \tfrac{\sqrt5}2\sin t\bigr)^{\top}}$.

  1. Show that $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ runs entirely within the contour line $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5}$.

  2. Determine $t$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,1)^{\top}}$, compute $\boldsymbol{\gamma}'(t)$ there and check that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma}'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$. Compare the direction with the vector $\mathbf{t}$ from Section 4.3.

  3. Use (6.4) to show in general: if a differentiable curve runs within a level set of $f$, then $\nabla f$ is perpendicular to its velocity vector.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t))}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5\cos^2t+4\cdot\tfrac54\sin^2t}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5\,(\cos^2t+\sin^2t)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5}$ for every $t$.

(b) From $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sqrt5\cos t}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\tfrac{\sqrt5}2\sin t}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$ it follows that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\cos t}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1/\sqrt5}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\sin t}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2/\sqrt5}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{t}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1.107}$. There,

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}'(t)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(-\sqrt5\sin t,\ \tfrac{\sqrt5}2\cos t\bigr)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(-2,\ \tfrac12\bigr)^{\top},}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(1,1)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma}'}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\cdot(-2)+8\cdot\tfrac12}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0 .}$$

The velocity vector is $-\tfrac12\,(4,\,-1)^{\top}$, so it is parallel to the vector $\mathbf{t}$ from Section 4.3.

(c) If $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ runs within the level set for the value $c$, then $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t))}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{c}$ for all $t$, and the derivative with respect to $t$ is zero. By (6.4), this derivative is $\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t))^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma}'(t)$. So the gradient is perpendicular to every velocity vector of a curve within the level set.

Go to the place in the article
Exercise 23 ★★★ A chain with three stages

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sum_{i=1}^2(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x})_i^2}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$. The calculation consists of three stages: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{u}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}$, then $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{v_i}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{u_i^2}$ for each component, then $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{v_1+v_2}$.

  1. Give the three Jacobian matrices at the point $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,1)^{\top}}$ and compute $\mathbf{J}_F$ as their product.

  2. Show in general that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla F(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}$, and check the result from (a).

  3. Compute the product from (a) once from the left and once from the right. Which order needs fewer multiplications if $\mathbf{A}$ is an $n\times n$ matrix?

Solution

(a) At the point $(2,\,1)$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{u}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(4,\,1)^{\top}}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{v}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(16,\,1)^{\top}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{17}$. The Jacobian matrices are $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_1}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\operatorname{diag}(2u_1,\,2u_2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\operatorname{diag}(8,\,2)}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_3}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1\ \ 1)}$. Their product is

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{J}_F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1\ \ 1)\,\operatorname{diag}(8,\,2)\,\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(8\ \ 2)\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(8\ \ 18).}$$

(b) Transposed, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\,\operatorname{diag}(2\mathbf{u})\,\mathbf{1}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\,2\mathbf{u}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}$. With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\2&5\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$, we get $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\,(4,\,9)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(8,\,18)^{\top}}$, as in (a). Check: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(x_1+2x_2)^2+x_2^2}$ has the partial derivatives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2\cdot4}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{8}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{4\cdot4+2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{18}$.

(c) From the left: $(1\ \ 1)\,\operatorname{diag}(8,2)$ is a row, for which you need $n$ multiplications, and the row times $\mathbf{A}$ needs $n^2$. From the right: $\operatorname{diag}(8,2)\,\mathbf{A}$ needs $n^2$ multiplications, and the row times the result another $n^2$. So from the left it takes about $n^2$ multiplications, from the right about $2n^2$. If the middle matrix were not diagonal but dense, the matrix product from the right would even cost about $n^3$. From the left, starting at the result, only rows ever arise.

Differentiating with respect to vectors and matrices

Exercise 24 ★ Gradients in matrix notation

Compute with the formulas of this section, and check each result with the partial derivatives.

  1. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+5}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{b}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,-1,\,4)^{\top}}$;

  2. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert^2}$ at the point $(1,\,-1,\,2)$;

  3. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\3&4\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$ at the point $(1,\,-1)$.

Solution

(a) By (7.1), $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{b}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,-1,\,4)^{\top}}$. Check: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x_1-x_2+4x_3+5}$.

(b) By (7.2) with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{I}}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,-2,\,4)^{\top}}$. Check: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_jf}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x_j}$.

(c) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4\\4&8\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(1,-1)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(4-4,\ 4-8)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0,\,-4)^{\top}}$. Check: $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x_1^2+4x_1x_2+4x_2^2}$ has the partial derivatives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{4x_1+4x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{4x_1+8x_2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-4}$.

Exercise 25 ★★ The least-squares line from Chapter 2

In Chapter 2, Section 3.6, a line was fitted through the points $(0,\,1)$, $(1,\,2)$ and $(2,\,4)$, with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\Bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\1&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\Bigr)}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{y}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2,\,4)^{\top}}$.

  1. Compute $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert^2}$ and $\nabla f(\mathbf{x})$ at the point $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,1)^{\top}}$.

  2. In which direction should you shift $\mathbf{x}$ a small step away from $(1,\,1)$ so that $f$ decreases fastest?

  3. Show that the gradient at the point $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\hat{\mathbf{x}}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\tfrac56,\,\tfrac32)^{\top}}$ from Chapter 2 is zero.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2,\,3)^{\top}}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{r}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0,\,0,\,1)^{\top}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1}$. With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{r}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0+0+1,\ 0+0+2)^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2)^{\top}}$, we get $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-2\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{r}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(-2,\,-4)^{\top}}$.

(b) $f$ decreases fastest in the direction $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{-\nabla f/\lVert\nabla f\rVert}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(2,\,4)^{\top}/\sqrt{20}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2)^{\top}/\sqrt5}\textcolor{#15633a}{\approx}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.447,\ 0.894)^{\top}}$: the intercept should grow a little, the slope twice as much. This fits the fact that the line $1+t$ misses the third point $(2,\,4)$ by $1$.

(c) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\tfrac56,\ \tfrac73,\ \tfrac{23}6)^{\top}}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\hat{\mathbf{r}}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\tfrac16,\ -\tfrac13,\ \tfrac16)^{\top}}$ and

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{r}}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac16-\tfrac13+\tfrac16\\0-\tfrac13+\tfrac26\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}0\\0\end{pmatrix}.}$$

The gradient is zero: at the best approximate solution, no small displacement changes the sum of squares to first order.

Exercise 26 ★★ Derivatives with respect to a matrix

  1. Show that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{I}}$ for square $\mathbf{A}$.

  2. Use (7.6) and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\mathbf{y}}$ to show that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\,(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x})\,\mathbf{x}^{\top}}$, and compute this for $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times2}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2)^{\top}}$.

  3. Check (b) with the linear approximation (7.4): by about how much does $\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert^2$ change if you increase $a_{12}$ from $0$ to $0.01$? Verify by calculation.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sum_ia_{ii}}$. The derivative with respect to $a_{ij}$ is $1$ for $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{i}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{j}$ and $0$ otherwise; arranged in the shape of $\mathbf{A}$, this is $\mathbf{I}$.

(b) We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{f(\mathbf{y})}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{y}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla f(\mathbf{y})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\mathbf{y}}$. By (7.6), $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\mathbf{y}\,\mathbf{x}^{\top}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\,(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x})\,\mathbf{x}^{\top}}$. For $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{I}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2)^{\top}}$:

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2\begin{pmatrix}1\\2\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&2\end{pmatrix}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}2&4\\4&8\end{pmatrix}.}$$

(c) The entry $(1,2)$ of the gradient is $4$, so (7.4) predicts a change of about $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{4\cdot0.01}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.04}$. Exactly: with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{a_{12}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0.01}$, we have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1.02,\ 2)^{\top}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert^2}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1.0404+4}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{5.0404}$, that is, a change of $0.0404$ compared with $5$.

Exercise 27 ★★★ The backward rule for a product of two matrices

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{C}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{f(\mathbf{C})}$ with $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf G}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}f}$.

  1. Show componentwise that $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf G\mathbf{B}^{\top}}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla_{\mathbf{B}}F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf G}$.

  2. Check both formulas for $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{A}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1\ \ 2)}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{B}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}3\\4\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{C})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{c_{11}^2}$ by writing out $F$.

Solution

(a) We have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{c_{ik}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sum_ja_{ij}b_{jk}}$. The entry $a_{ij}$ acts on all $c_{ik}$ of row $i$, with the factor $b_{jk}$; summed over these paths, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial F/\partial a_{ij}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sum_kg_{ik}b_{jk}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\mathbf G\mathbf{B}^{\top})_{ij}}$. The entry $b_{jk}$ acts on all $c_{ik}$ of column $k$, with the factor $a_{ij}$; so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial F/\partial b_{jk}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\sum_ia_{ij}g_{ik}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf G)_{jk}}$.

(b) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{C}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1\cdot3+2\cdot4}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{11}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{121}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf G}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2c_{11}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{22}$. The formulas give $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{22\,(3\ \ 4)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(66\ \ 88)}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\nabla_{\mathbf{B}}F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(1,\,2)^{\top}\cdot22}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(22,\,44)^{\top}}$. Written out, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(3a_{11}+4a_{12})^2}$, with the derivatives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2\cdot11\cdot3}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{66}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2\cdot11\cdot4}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{88}$, and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{F}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(b_{11}+2b_{21})^2}$, with the derivatives $22$ and $44$.

The Hessian matrix and the quadratic approximation

Exercise 28 ★ Hessian matrices

Compute the Hessian matrix at the given point and check its symmetry.

  1. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1^3+x_1x_2^2}$ at the point $(1,\,2)$;

  2. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathrm{e}^{x_1+2x_2}}$ at the point $(0,\,0)$. What is the rank of the matrix?

  3. $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1x_2x_3}$ at the point $(1,\,2,\,3)$.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{3x_1^2+x_2^2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x_1x_2}$, so

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{H}_f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}6x_1&2x_2\\2x_2&2x_1\end{pmatrix},}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{H}_f(1,2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}6&4\\4&2\end{pmatrix};}$$

the mixed derivatives $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2(3x_1^2+x_2^2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x_2}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1(2x_1x_2)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2x_2}$ are equal.

(b) With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathrm{e}^{x_1+2x_2}}$, we have $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{f}$ and $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{2f}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{H}_f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{f\cdot\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\2&4\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$, and at the origin $\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\2&4\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$. The second row is twice the first; the rank is $1$, and the eigenvalues are $5$ and $0$. This fits: $f$ depends only on $x_1+2x_2$ and is constant along the direction $(2,\,-1)^{\top}$, so it is not curved there.

(c) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_2x_3}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1x_3}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_3f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1x_2}$, so

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{H}_f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}0&x_3&x_2\\x_3&0&x_1\\x_2&x_1&0\end{pmatrix},}$$
$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{H}_f(1,2,3)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\begin{pmatrix}0&3&2\\3&0&1\\2&1&0\end{pmatrix}.}$$
Go to the place in the article
Exercise 29 ★★ Quadratic approximation

Let $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{f(\mathbf{x})}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1\,\mathrm{e}^{x_2}}$ as in Exercise 10.

  1. Compute $\mathbf{H}_f(2,0)$.

  2. Use the quadratic approximation at the point $(2,\,0)$ to compute an approximation for $f(2.1,\ -0.05)$, and compare it with the linear approximation from Exercise 10 and with the exact value.

Solution

(a) $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1^2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{0}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_1\partial_2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\mathrm{e}^{x_2}}$, $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\partial_2^2f}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{x_1\mathrm{e}^{x_2}}$, so $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{H}_f(2,0)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}$.

(b) With $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\mathbf{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{(0.1,\ -0.05)^{\top}}$,

$$\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{\tfrac12\,\mathbf{h}^{\top}\mathbf{H}_f\mathbf{h}}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac12\bigl(0+2\cdot1\cdot0.1\cdot(-0.05)+2\cdot0.05^2\bigr)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{\tfrac12\,(-0.01+0.005)}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{-0.0025 .}$$

The quadratic approximation is $\textcolor{#1b7f4a}{2+0-0.0025}\textcolor{#15633a}{=}\textcolor{#c2410c}{1.9975}$, the linear one was $2.0$, and the exact value is $1.997582$. The error drops from $0.0024$ to $0.00008$. The cross term $-0.005$ is the interaction from Exercise 10.
